## Message 63

- **ID:** 63

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 68

### Text

🔐 $11M Cork Protocol Hack Exposed Critical Uniswap V4 Hook Flaws

On May 28, 2025, Cork Protocol lost $11M due to missing access control in their Uniswap V4 hook contract. The attacker exploited poorly protected beforeSwap calls, tricking the protocol into crediting fake token deposits.

Key issues:
- No restriction on who could call hook functions
- Allowed cross-market token confusion (using insurance tokens as safe tokens)
- No pool validation or whitelisting
- Token states overlapped across markets (Singleton pattern)

Despite multiple audits, this vulnerability slipped through, showing the complexity of Uniswap V4 hooks security.

Takeaway:  
Always enforce strict onlyPoolManager access and validate tokens and pools carefully when building hooks on Uniswap V4.

Read the full tech deep dive 👉 here

### Links

- https://dedaub.com/blog/the-11m-cork-protocol-hack-a-critical-lesson-in-uniswap-v4-hook-security/

## Message 79

- **ID:** 79
- **Date (UTC):** 2025-06-11T15:58:29+00:00
- **Date (WAT, Africa/Lagos):** 2025-06-11T16:58:29+01:00
- **URL:** https://t.me/defendor_eng/79
- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Views:** 50

### Text

🛡️ Critical AMM Bug Allows Hidden Rug Pull via Duplicate Pool Creation

A gas-optimized AMM called ZAMM lets attackers create the same coin/pool pair multiple times in one second, hiding LP tokens to later rug the pool. 

This happens because pool IDs depend only on user address + timestamp, with no reads to detect overwrites—letting attackers steal funds unnoticed.

Regular pools are safe; only AMM-created coins are vulnerable.

Quick fix: ZAMM hid duplicates in UI and added docs.

🛠️ Prevention: Use a global nonce or check existing pools before creation.

Detect via fuzzing and supply ≤ balance checks—unit tests often miss this.

Read full post 👉 here

### Links

- https://t.co/b6IDUtHyu7

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Views:** 57

### Text

🔐 EVM Calldata Decoded: Essential for DeFi Security  

Understanding raw calldata—the encoded inputs sent to Ethereum smart contracts—is key to decoding transactions and spotting anomalies.  

- Calldata consists of static (fixed size) and dynamic (variable size) parameters.  
- The first 4 bytes identify the function selector, guiding the EVM which function to run.  
- Dynamic types start with an offset + length, followed by their data.  
Mastering this lets you audit, debug, and build safer contracts.  

Read full guide 👉 here

### Links

- https://degatchi.com/articles/reading-raw-evm-calldata

🔍 EulerSwap Code Insight: Potential Security Flags for DeFi Devs

The new EulerSwap code passes a lot of user-configured params appended to calldata, not explicitly in function signatures—this raises security sensors.  

Good news: all critical functions have built-in nonReentrantView checks, reducing risks and gas costs.  

However, during swaps, collateral temporarily undercollateralizes the backend platform, which could be risky.  

Stay alert and read the full post for details!

### Text

🔥 Mastering BLS Signatures: Do’s & Don’ts

BLS signatures power Ethereum and EigenLayer but can be tricky to use right.

Why BLS? Their magic is signature aggregation—combine many signatures into one for faster, cheaper on-chain verification.

EigenLayer uses this smartly, letting validators sign tasks and aggregate their signatures securely on-chain.

Beware: improper use risks rogue-key attacks, where attackers fake multi-signatures without others’ consent.

Want the full breakdown and how to avoid pitfalls?  
🔗 Full details

🚨 $2M UniBTC Hack Linked to Former Fuzzland Employee

Fuzzland revealed that a previous employee orchestrated a $2M exploit on Bedrock’s UniBTC protocol in Sept 2024. 

Using social engineering and supply chain attacks, the insider implanted malicious code that enabled backdoors in engineering systems. This access allowed the attacker to exploit a known vulnerability — one that was initially flagged but deprioritized due to false positives. 

- No client data was compromised; the breach was isolated internally  
- Fuzzland has compensated Bedrock and is investigating with ZeroShadow  
- Reports were filed with Chinese and US authorities  
- The incident reflects a growing shift from smart contract hacks to social engineering attacks  

Despite the loss, Bedrock's TVL surged from $240M to $535M by mid-2025.

### Text

🚀 Solidity Precision Tip

In Solidity, always multiply before dividing to keep full precision. 

• Using 1e18 simulates decimals since Solidity has no real decimals.  
• This makes 100% equal to 1e18.  
• For example, 25% is represented as 2.5e17.  

Follow this to avoid precision loss in calculations.

🔗 Full details

### Text

🚨 Wormhole Bridge $320M Hack Explained

On Feb 2, 2022, Wormhole Bridge on Solana suffered a massive hack due to a smart contract vulnerability, losing ~120,000 wETH (~$320M).  

The attacker exploited a weak verify_signatures function by injecting a fake sysvar account, bypassing security to mint unauthorized wETH.  

• Initial fees came from Tornado Cash, hiding attack origins  
• Stolen assets crossed from Solana to Ethereum, then swapped for USDC and SOL  
• The attacker still holds over $297M in crypto today  

This case highlights how deprecated functions and poor sysvar validation create critical risks in cross-chain bridges.  

Always audit your smart contracts with trusted experts to prevent such breaches.  

🔗 Full details

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Views:** 108

### Text

🚨 BNB Bridge Hack Explained

In Oct 2022, the BNB Bridge was hacked due to a flaw in its Merkle proof verification. The bug arose because path nodes could have both Left and Right flags set, allowing attackers to inject malicious transfers. 

• The bridge only kept one root hash on-chain, relying on user-controlled data for proofs.  
• The vulnerability let attackers bypass verification by exploiting inconsistent hash calculations.  
• Fixes required aligning side checks for root and subsequent leaf proofs.  
• No library patch was made; BNB Chain fixed nodes instead.  

This hack highlights the risks of gas optimization and the importance of consistent verification in Web3 security.

🔗 Full details

### Links

- https://composable-security.com/blog/bnb-bridge-hack-eli5-explained-and-visualised/

### Text

🛡️ Cross-Chain NFT Sync Glitch Exposed

  In a recent audit for Yuga Labs, a risky edge case in their lzRead integration was uncovered.  
  This bug could silently freeze NFT syncing across all chains using LayerZero v2.  

• lzRead relies on DVNs to verify on-chain reads; if a read reverts, verification fails.  
• Failed verification causes message blocking and stalls the entire cross-chain pipeline.  
• The issue arose from a function that reverted when NFTs were locked, halting syncing indefinitely.  
• Recommended fix: return structured data instead of reverting to keep DVNs verifying smoothly.  
• Yuga Labs applied the fix prior to launch, showcasing the need for protocol-aware audits.  

This case highlights the complexity of cross-chain messaging and importance of deep integration testing.

🔗 Details

### Text

🛡️ Critical Solana Vulnerability: Remote Code Execution on Validators

In Solana v1.16, a bug was discovered in the Direct Mapping optimization that allows changing memory pointers of validators and gaining full RCE. This threatened the security of a network with a TVL of over $9 billion. The vulnerability is related to an error in host memory updating, which enables attacks through pointer substitution in CPI.

The exploit is based on out-of-bounds read/write via modified buffer sizes and clever memory manipulations, providing arbitrary read/write and potential RCE.

Solana has already suspended Direct Mapping and continues working on a fix, confirming the seriousness of the issue and a responsible response.

The highest level of security is achieved through radical immutability and the rejection of centralized administration.

### Text

🛡️ Replay Attacks in Blockchain: What You Need to Know

Replay attacks allow attackers to reuse signed transactions to steal funds or manipulate data. The main vulnerabilities include the absence of a unique nonce, attacks during hard forks, cross-chain replays, and signature forgery due to their malleability.

Protection relies on the mandatory use of unique nonces, expiration checks, limiting signature values, and verifying the chain ID.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.cyfrin.io/blog/replay-attack-in-ethereum

🛡️ Critical Vulnerability in NEAR Protocol P2P Network

A bug was found in the NEAR network layer that allows sending a single malicious handshake to crash any node in the network, stopping the entire blockchain system.

The issue is related to improper handling of SECP256K1 signatures — due to the lack of data length verification and an incorrect recovery ID value, a panic occurs leading to node failure.

The vulnerability has already been fixed, and $150,000 was paid as a bounty for its discovery in NEAR.

### Text

🌳 Security of Merkle Roots in Web3

Merkle trees and state roots are important for blockchains, but verifying them on the L2 layer is a challenging task.

You must always verify the L2 state root through a trusted contract, signature, or nested confirmation on L1.

Without reliable verification, your Merkle proof is just a guess, and the key to security is a source with authentic data.

🔗 Details

Through this loophole, it is possible to launch MEV attacks, including front-running, on major DEXs such as ZetaChain and SushiSwap.

The issue is related to improper configuration of cloud logging infrastructure, leading to leakage of critical data and API keys.

This is an example of how Web2 problems affect the security of Web3.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://0d.dwalletlabs.com/breaching-ethereums-privacy-and-exploiting-dexs-using-a-simple-cloud-vulnerability-7f2aa4beaec7

Ethereum offers broad integration and a mature ecosystem, but high gas costs and immutable pools can create risks in case of errors.

In both solutions, balancing capital efficiency and system security is essential.

### Text

🛡️ Fraudulent Solidity Extension Stole $500K from a Developer

In June 2025, a malicious plugin for Cursor AI with 54,000 downloads disguised itself as a Solidity syntax highlighter but downloaded and executed PowerShell scripts to install remote control.

The attackers exploited the Open VSX ranking algorithm, which promoted their fake extension above the original one that had fewer downloads.

Using a backdoor and stealer, they stole private keys and drained cryptocurrency. The fake extension was quickly removed, but a new one appeared with 2 million downloads and a similar name.

Such attacks on open-source packages are on the rise, threatening the security of Web3 developers.

🔗 Details

### Text

🛡️ Quantum Attack on Web3 Is Already Near

The cryptography of most smart contracts and wallets based on ECC is vulnerable to quantum computing and Shor’s attack.

The attack is possible even years later — attackers are already collecting encrypted data to crack it later on powerful quantum machines.

Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) is the new standard for protection, which has already been approved by NIST and will soon become mandatory for Web3.

Auditors are checking protocols for quantum vulnerability, crypto-adaptability, and support for hybrid signatures.

🔗 Details

💥 13 Billion RMB Disappeared: The Collapse of the Xinkangjia DGCX Scam Platform

The Xinkangjia DGCX platform froze withdrawals, deceiving over 2 million investors and stealing approximately 13 billion RMB. 

The project used fake contracts, a 9-level MLM system, and promised unrealistically high returns of up to 2% per day. 

On-chain analysis revealed a classic Ponzi scheme with centralized control of funds and hidden commissions of up to 50% on withdrawals. 

Russian and Chinese regulators had warned about the risks, but the platform continued actively attracting investors through deception and fakes. 

The collapse of Xinkangjia is a vivid example of the threats in Web3 from services with fake licenses and aggressive marketing.

### Text

🛡️ Why Wallet UX Is Setting Up Web3 Users for Failure

Signature requests in MetaMask and other wallets often display confusing hex data, misleading users.

Signature attacks are not code bugs, but social engineering and poor UX that can cause users to lose assets by signing malicious transactions.

Many dApps and wallets don’t explain the meaning of signatures, allowing scammers to gain broad permissions to manage tokens and NFTs.

The problem is that users click "Sign" without understanding the risks — opening the door to attacks without any complex hacks.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://blog.immunebytes.com/2025/07/21/the-signature-trap-why-wallet-ux-is-failing-users-in-web3/

🛡️ Sui Move: A New Model for Secure Contracts

Sui uses objects with unique IDs and explicit ownership, unlike EVM and Solana. This simplifies rights management and reduces the risk of data forgery.

Decentralized access control in Move is built on "capabilities" — special objects that must be owned to perform an action. This ensures compile-time security without complex runtime checks.

### Text

🛡️ An error with block.chainid can break permit() in EIP-2612

Contracts that store block.chainid at deployment time to calculate the DOMAIN_SEPARATOR stop working after a hard fork.

This leads to signature verification failures and funds becoming locked.

The best solution is to recalculate the DOMAIN_SEPARATOR dynamically to avoid these issues.

Use well-audited libraries like OpenZeppelin, which take these nuances into account.

🔗 Details

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Views:** 163

### Text

🛡️ Attack via EIP-7702: New Risks for Web3

An attacker used EIP-7702 to mask calls and steal about 0.4 ETH (~$1400) from a victim’s contract.

EIP-7702 allows EOAs (externally owned accounts) to execute code like smart contracts, bypassing the check msg.sender == tx.origin.

Because of this, the protection of the contract was broken, which trusted input data and allowed token transfers to any address.

The attack shows that new standards can create unexpected security vulnerabilities.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://x.com/TikkalaResearch/status/1947889474987561271

Developing a personal bug classification and adjusting your approach increases audit effectiveness without relying on memory.

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 179

### Text

👑 Dangers of Transferring Lamports in Solana

Transferring lamports to arbitrary accounts can cause program failures due to hidden restrictions: accounts must remain rent-exempt, not be executable, or reserved.  

Even if an account is marked writable, it can be automatically downgraded to read-only, which will block operations and render the contract nonfunctional.  

It is better to avoid direct transfers, storing returns in a PDA storage instead to protect against unpredictable changes and bugs.  

These nuances have already caused real problems in DeFi and require careful attention when developing secure Solana smart contracts.  

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://osec.io/blog/2025-05-14-king-of-the-sol

🔒 Polocolo — an efficient ZK-friendly hash function

Polocolo is a new ZK-friendly hash function using lookup tables and the power residue method. It reduces the load on PLONK gates by 21–24% compared to current solutions.

Lookup gates save resources on complex operations, such as XOR, making proofs more efficient.

Polocolo addresses the costly processing of base decomposition, providing faster and more secure performance in ZK protocols.

🛡 Risks and Vulnerabilities of Upgradeable Smart Contracts

Upgradeable contracts often suffer from issues such as uninitialized proxies, storage collisions, and dangerous upgrades. These mistakes allow attackers to seize control, reset configurations, or "mint" infinite tokens.

Known cases:  
Wormhole (2022) — vulnerability due to missed initialization;  
AllianceBlock (2024) — reinitialization with token alteration;  
Audius (2022) — storage collision allowed contract reinitialization.

Errors in upgrades and access control are common attack vectors, even if the logic code itself is flawless.

🔐 Sequencing Security in Ethereum and Rollups

Sequencing is responsible for the ordering and execution of transactions in Layer 2, impacting censorship resistance and MEV transparency.

Different Rollup models require comprehensive protection: from smart contracts to off-chain logic and upgrade management.

Errors in batching and proof verification can lead to invalid state before the end of the challenge window.

New shared sequencers complicate security due to the interaction of multiple participants.

Full protection is an ongoing process involving design review, implementation, and post-launch monitoring.

When developing a Uniswap v4 hook, it is important to consider support for multiple pools and state management to avoid conflicts and data loss.

Protective access control mechanisms must prevent unauthorized calls and manage updates to avoid attacks costing $10M+.

- **Views:** 154

### Text

🛡️ Cyclic Groups and the Discrete Logarithm in Cryptography

A cyclic group is a set of elements generated by repeatedly applying an operation to a single element — the generator.  

The security of many protocols is based on the discrete logarithm problem — finding the exponent of the generator that produces a given element, which is considered a computationally hard problem.  

Using subgroups of prime order enhances the security and efficiency of cryptography and zero-knowledge proofs, allowing secret values to be hidden without revealing the information itself.  

Cryptography relies on the hardness of solving the discrete logarithm problem in cyclic groups, which ensures the protection of private keys and the anonymity of data.  

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.defendor.xyz/cyclic-groups-discrete-log-problem-explained-the-math-behind-cryptography-security/

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 146

### Text

🛡️ Critical Vulnerability in Permit2: Irreversible DoS Griefing

The SignatureVerification::verify function calls claimedSigner::isValidSignature for contracts, which is generally safe. However, if a contract uses Permit2 with permitWitnessTransferFrom and generates its transaction ID based on non-substitutable data, an attacker can harm all users.

The attacker creates a contract with a signature that is always valid, changes the amount to 1 wei, and substitutes the user address. This causes a DoS where honest transactions keep reverting while the attacker’s costs remain minimal.

🛡️ Qubic and the "51% Attack" on Monero: Hype or Threat?

Qubic claimed to have taken control of 51% of Monero’s hashrate and launched an attack with block reorgs, but independent data shows only about ~40% of the power and limited disruptions.

Miners moved to Qubic motivated by triple profits, but then the community sharply turned away, reducing their share to 15%.

Actual criticism was also directed at the secrecy and the disabling of the reporting API — it seems this was a calculated marketing move aimed at boosting the price of QUBIC tokens.

There is no real damage to the network, but the incident revealed the vulnerability of PoW networks to financial incentive pressures, not just technical attacks.

- **Views:** 166

### Text

⚠️ Critical Vulnerability in RISC Zero zkVM

In versions 2.0.0–2.0.2, an important constraint is missing in the rv32im scheme, allowing an attacker to create false proofs of code execution.

This violates soundness — the foundation of trust in zero-knowledge proofs — making it possible to accept mathematically incorrect results.

The vulnerability affects key RISC-V instructions and risks undermining trust in applications based on this technology.

🔗 Details

- https://www.defendor.xyz/risc-zero-zkvm-hit-by-critical-missing-constraint-flaw-allowing-false-proofs/

🛡 Uniswap V4 Hooks: More Power — More Risks

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 123

### Text

🛡️ WebView Security in Mobile Crypto Wallets

WebView is an embedded browser within an app that allows decentralized applications (dApps) to interact with the wallet.

Main threats include address bar spoofing, where the user sees a fake URL, and attacks on the communication bridge between the WebView and the app.

It is also possible for a malicious iframe to be embedded inside the WebView, forging requests or intercepting responses, thereby threatening the security of funds.

Implementation requires strict separation of trusted UI and web content, careful verification of the message source, and protection against race conditions when sending data.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.defendor.xyz/securing-mobile-crypto-wallets-how-to-safely-use-webviews-without-losing-user-trust/

- **Views:** 109

### Text

🧩 Homomorphisms — the foundation of zero-knowledge

A homomorphism is a function that preserves the structure of mathematical operations when transitioning from secret data to its encrypted form or commitments.

This allows performing computations on hidden data without revealing it, which is critical for zero-knowledge proofs.

In protocols like PLONK, operations on commitments mirror operations on the original values, maintaining confidentiality and integrity.

This way, complex computations can be proven through simple checks without disclosing the data itself.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.defendor.xyz/zk-math-101-how-homomorphisms-power-zero-knowledge-proofs/

- **Views:** 117

### Text

⚙️ EIP-7702: Smart Functions for All Ethereum Accounts

EIP-7702 allows regular Ethereum accounts (EOAs) to temporarily delegate control to smart contracts, combining the simplicity of EOAs with the flexibility of contracts.

This solves the limitations of EOAs: one operation per transaction, mandatory ETH for gas, and lack of advanced security, without requiring the creation of new wallets.

New features include transaction batching, gas sponsorship, and permission management, but new risks related to delegation and code security also arise.

🔗 Details

- https://www.defendor.xyz/eip-7702-unlocking-smart-contract-power-for-every-ethereum-account/

### Text

🔢 The Danger of Integer Overflow and Underflow in Web3

Integer overflow and underflow are arithmetic errors that occur when numbers exceed the limits of their designated types, compromising data integrity in smart contracts.

This leads to distorted balances, function freezes, and system manipulation, undermining trust in DeFi protocols.

They often happen due to disabled protections in Solidity or mistakes with type casting.

Reliable implementation of arithmetic is key to the security and stability of Web3 applications.

🔗 Details

🦈 Phishing and $13 Million: How Venus Protocol Saved a Wealthy User

The owner of a large deposit on Venus Protocol fell into a trap during a Zoom call and signed a malicious transaction, losing $13 million in 6 seconds.

Venus urgently froze the protocol and launched a vote to forcibly liquidate the attacker’s assets — this case reveals the centralized safety switch behind the facade of decentralization.

Within 12 hours, the protocol returned the funds, demonstrating that in DeFi, emergency powers often matter more than code and democracy.

The victim was targeted by hackers from Lazarus Group — an example of the terrifying power of state-sponsored attacks on users, even those with hardware wallets.

### Text

🛡️ A Simple Fix to Protect Against Lazarus Attacks on Multisig

Over 18 months, hackers from North Korea, including the Lazarus group, stole more than $1.75 billion by forging signatures in multisig wallets.

A flaw in the transaction confirmation process allows attackers to trick multiple participants into signing malicious actions.

Expert Daniel Von Fange suggests adding a delay before transaction execution with the option to cancel — a sort of "undo button" to protect funds.

This simple step could prevent repeated large-scale thefts and save billions.

🔗 Details

- https://www.defendor.xyz/simple-fix-could-stop-cryptos-1-75b-lazarus-multisig-hacks/

### Text

🛡 Nemo Hack: $2.6M Leak Due to Code and Audit Errors

On September 7, the Nemo protocol lost about $2.59 million due to two vulnerabilities: a public flash_loan function intended for internal use, and a faulty function that changed the contract’s state without authorization.

The error occurred because a new, unaudited function was introduced after the initial audit, combined with weak controls during the contract upgrade via a single-signature wallet.

The attack was quickly detected, the protocol was paused, vulnerabilities were fixed, and joint efforts with Security Services and law enforcement have begun.

🔗 Details

### Text

🏦 Vulnerabilities in Permissioned Capital Market Smart Contracts

Permissioned Capital Market (PCM) protocols in TradFi differ significantly from DeFi: they include only verified participants with KYC, have administrators with the right to lock assets, and enforce strict regulatory compliance.

In PCM, data tracking errors and admin function issues are common, leading to rule violations and hidden failures that are difficult to detect.

There are also identified privilege vulnerabilities, inaccuracies in financial calculations, and risks in cross-chain communication — all of which undermine protocol security.

🔗 Details

### Text

🛡️ SwissBorg lost $41.5 million due to a hack of a partner’s API

Partner Kiln provided hackers with keys to withdraw funds, leading to the theft of 192.6K SOL within minutes.

The breach was hidden within routine unstaking instructions, which allowed the attackers to gain full control.

SwissBorg promises compensation to those affected, but the incident highlights the dangers of delegating security to third-party APIs.

### Text

🌉 Polygon Security: Risks of PoS and zkEVM

Polygon combines a PoS sidechain with zkEVM, but each layer has its own risks.

The PoS bridge is vulnerable due to centralized validators and insufficient protection at the Ethereum layer.

zkEVM provides reliable proofs but is still centralized and controlled by the Council, with fast upgrades executed without a timelock.

Errors in zk-chains can lead to incorrect state transitions, which is an important consideration for developers.

Polygon’s hybrid architecture requires special attention to bridges, validators, and governance to reduce systemic risks.

🔗 Details

- https://www.defendor.xyz/securing-polygon-essential-risk-guide-for-pos-zkevm-deployments/

### Text

🎲 Security of Random Number Generation on Solana

Generating randomness on Solana is challenging due to the need for all validators to obtain identical results.

Built-in methods using SlotHashes and the Clock Sysvar are entirely predictable and can be compromised by validators.

Popular services like Switchboard VRF and ORAO VRF offer verifiable and reliable randomness, minimizing the risk of manipulation.

Part 2 will discuss the advantages and risks of such RNG services for secure applications.

🔗 Details

- https://www.defendor.xyz/on-chain-randomness-on-solana-why-native-methods-are-predictable-safer-alternatives-part-1/

- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 266

### Text

🐍 Solana Security Basics: From EVM to SVM

Solana stores data in Program Derived Addresses (PDAs) — separate accounts with strict access controls that are crucial for security.

Cross-Program Invocation (CPI) allows programs to interact but requires verification of the correctness of PDA signatures.

SPL tokens differ from ERC-20 — balances are stored in separate Token Accounts, which affects management and security.

Transactions on Solana are atomic and size-limited, protecting against partial failures and attacks.

SOL gives programs access to the entire account balance, so control and restrictions are critical to prevent losses.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.defendor.xyz/transitioning-from-evm-to-solana-essential-security-concepts-you-need-to-know/

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security

- **Views:** 217

### Text

⛓️ Risk in Ethereum Liquid Staking: Vulnerability with Validator Deposits

In the Ethereum liquid staking system, the first deposit transaction sets the withdrawal address for all subsequent deposits to that validator.

If an attacker makes the minimal deposit first with their own withdrawal credentials, they will gain control over the withdrawals of all subsequent deposits.

MEV solutions do not help in this case, and reliable protection involves using protocol-controlled validators and making the initial deposit when creating the validator.

Off-chain deposit state verification and multisignature schemes are additional measures but require complexity in implementation.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.defendor.xyz/ethereum-liquid-staking-risks-how-validator-deposit-flaws-can-cost-you-funds-and-how-to-stop-it/

- **Views:** 209

### Text

🛡️ Security in Modular Blockchains

Modular protocols distribute functions across different layers: sequencers, verifiers, data availability layers, and executors. 

Security depends on the coordinated operation of these components, as a failure or attack at one level can affect the entire system.

Centralization of sequencers and verifiers carries risks of censorship and delays, while the data availability layer must guarantee complete publication of information.

Coordinated upgrade management and transparent infrastructure are critical for maintaining trust and stability.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.defendor.xyz/security-coordination-in-modular-blockchains-essential-guide-for-developers-and-institutions/

### Text

🔗 Web3 Security: Risks of Protocol Composability

Composability is a key feature of Web3 that combines protocols but creates new vulnerabilities when they interact.

Errors often occur not within individual contracts, but in their connections, leading to large-scale failures and hacks.

To protect against this, it is important to assess dependencies, model behavior, and maintain clear documentation of integrations.

Projects should design systems so that any external interactions are controllable and secure.

🔗 Details

- https://www.defendor.xyz/beyond-audits-securing-web3-protocols-against-composability-risks/

- **Views:** 199

### Text

🛡 Oracle and Pricing Risks in Smart Contracts

Using oracles and price feeds in DeFi often leads to vulnerabilities such as price manipulation via flash loans, liquidity issues, and front-running.

The most common mistakes are relying on the spot price, lacking timing protection, and improper data aggregation. All of these open the door to attacks and losses.

It is important to consider update delays, mathematical or parameter defects, and to avoid hardcoded price assumptions.

For complex oracles, it is better to use multiple sources and median values to make manipulation more difficult.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.defendor.xyz/common-oracle-pricing-risks-in-smart-contracts-how-to-spot-and-prevent-costly-attacks/

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 176

### Text

🛡️ TON Security: Key Features and Risks

TON is a unique blockchain with an asynchronous smart contract execution model, which eliminates classic reentrancy vulnerabilities.

Data is stored in cells — a structure with size limitations, simplifying logic and enhancing security.

However, due to the asynchronous nature, it is important to properly handle bounced messages; otherwise, loss of control over the state and vulnerabilities may occur.

There are no EOA (Externally Owned Account) addresses in TON — all accounts are contracts, so address validation and wallet code verification are critically important for asset protection.

Overflow, division by zero, and data serialization errors are still relevant and require careful attention.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.defendor.xyz/ton-security-primer-understanding-key-features-and-unique-blockchain-risks/

### Text

🛡 Balancer v2 Vulnerability Analysis and Lessons for Web3

In November 2025, an attack occurred on Balancer v2 Composable Stable Pools, resulting in losses exceeding $120 million.

The root cause was a rounding error in the scaling function, caused by the override of _scalingFactors, which became possible in the new pools after OpenZeppelin audits.  

The attacker exploited low liquidity and the batchSwap mechanism to repeatedly reset the fractional part of the swap, profiting from incorrect calculations.

The contract has two branches: SET_IMPLEMENTATION and DELEGATE.

For the exploit, a contract with bytecode 0x33ff is needed, which sends all ETH to the sender using CALLER and SELFDESTRUCT.

This simple trick allowed winning the CTF and claiming 0.1 ETH.

- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 281

### Text

🧮 Balancer: $128M Lost Due to Rounding Error

On November 11, Balancer V2 was attacked exploiting a rounding error that ignored the bidirectionality of the scaling function, allowing the theft of tokens through complex multipools.

Several networks were targeted: Ethereum (~$91.5M), Arbitrum, Base, Optimism, Polygon, and forks; the total damage amounted to about $128 million.

The code was audited, but the arithmetic vulnerability went unnoticed, and replicating the attack across forks turned the issue into a large-scale crisis.

Some funds were frozen and recovered, but approximately $37 million remain with the attackers.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://rekt.news/balancer-rekt2

### Text

🛡️ North Korea Uses EtherHiding for Stealthy Infection

Since 2025, the UNC5342 group has been employing EtherHiding — a technique that stores malicious code in smart contracts on Ethereum and BNB Smart Chain.

This enables bypassing blocks, making the infection persistent and difficult to track.

The attack begins with social engineering: victims receive fake job offers and malicious tasks, after which malware is loaded into their browsers that then downloads exploits from the blockchain.

The malware steals data, crypto wallets, and is controlled through the blockchain, complicating detection.

🛡️ UNC5142 Uses Web3 to Distribute Malware

Since late 2023, the UNC5142 group has been infecting vulnerable WordPress sites by hiding malicious code in smart contracts on the BNB Smart Chain using the EtherHiding technique.

The attack is built on a three-level contract system, making it more flexible and resilient to takedowns. Infostealers (VIDAR, ATOMIC, RADTHIEF) are being distributed, while malicious landing pages are disguised as legitimate Cloudflare pages.

Using blockchain complicates detection and provides attackers with fast infrastructure updates at low costs.

### Text

🛡️ How to Find Dangerous Vulnerabilities in Web3 Code

Beginners often get lost in thousands of lines of code and search for bugs everywhere.

In reality, it is important to identify 2–3 key flows where security is determined and money moves.  

Focus on the logic of these flows and analyze who and what can control them.

This way, you will quickly spot where dangers may be hiding — without auditing the entire codebase.  

This approach helps to systematically and efficiently find critical vulnerabilities.  

🔗 Details

### Text

🛡️ EIP-7702: Whitelist Bypass Vulnerability

EIP-7702 allows EOAs (Externally Owned Addresses) to operate like smart contracts, which enables new possibilities but also opens attack vectors.

Through delegated calls via proxies, an attacker can bypass whitelist restrictions by using delegated authority from a trusted address.

The primary defense is to ensure that calls originate directly from EOAs, not through delegation.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.halborn.com/blog/post/eip-7702-security-considerations

- **Views:** 404

### Text

🌉 Curve Finance Fundamentals: Key Mechanics for Web3 Security

Curve v1 uses StableSwap to reduce slippage between stable assets, while Curve v2 introduced CryptoSwap, which concentrates liquidity for volatile pairs. crvUSD adds LLAMMA, enabling soft and gradual liquidations.

Security incidents include the July 2023 exploit caused by a Vyper compiler bug that broke re-entrancy guards, and a 2022 read-only re-entrancy issue tied to oracle behavior.

When auditing Curve-based systems, pay attention to iterative math, dynamic parameters, and protections against oracle manipulation.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.zealynx.io/blogs/curve-finance-core-mechanics

### Text

🛡️ Analysis of the $9M Attack on Yearn Finance

The hack occurred due to a rounding error that allowed the creation of an almost infinite amount of yETH, similar to a vulnerability in Balancer.

The second factor was the update_rate function, which caused losses of about 1000 ETH per call and amplified the total damage.

A calculation error of the invariant played a key role alongside the simple rounding error.

🔗 Details

### Text

🔐 OAuth Vulnerabilities in Exchanges: Risks of Account Takeover

The research revealed misconfigured OAuth and CORS settings that allow attackers to hijack accounts without user interaction.

The risk is associated with trusted localhost and http subdomains, which make it easier to bypass sandboxes in mobile apps and carry out MITM attacks.

Issues were found in Web3Auth Mobile SDK, Slush Wallet, Kukai Wallet, and other platforms; all vulnerabilities have been promptly fixed.

The key to security is to separate development and production environments and to exclude localhost and http from production configurations.

🔗 Details

### Text

🔒 Silent Protocol: Solving the State Denial Problem in Web3

In blockchains, user data is public, which breaches privacy. 

Silent Protocol shifts confidentiality to the user side while preserving composability and verifiability.

It splits transactions into independent parts, executing them publicly and returning the results to a private Ghost Layer storage.

This allows maintaining the publicly accessible state of applications without compromising privacy.

This achieves a balance between transparency, privacy, and the interoperability of smart contracts.

### Text

⛔ Critical VeChain Vulnerability: Delegation Freeze

Any delegation added in the same period when a validator signals an exit becomes permanently frozen due to a logic handling error.

Attempting to withdraw funds after the validator’s exit leads to a balance reset again, which causes an overflow error and blocks the transaction.

Fixing this requires a hard fork of the VeChain network, and the current situation poses a serious security risk for stakers.

🔗 Details

- https://reports.immunefi.com/vechain-hayabusa-upgrade-or-attackathon/55632-bc-critical-delegation-submitted-in-the-same-period-before-a-validator-exit-will-be-permanentl#delegation-added-in-period-5

### Text

🛡 Why High Bug Bounties Won't Stop Hacks

High bug bounties are a passive defense that relies on luck: whether a white-hat or black-hat hacker will find a bug.

The best approach is regular re-audits, which actively identify vulnerabilities and maintain security, unlike one-time rewards.

Increasing bug bounties creates a conflict of interest with researchers and does not guarantee the security of large protocols.

Audits provide a targeted assessment of current security, but they become outdated and require updates to protect against new threats.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://samczsun.com/higher-bug-bounties-wont-stop-hacks/

### Text

🚨 Issue with Prysm on Ethereum Mainnet

On December 4, 2025, almost all Prysm nodes experienced resource overload due to the validation of invalid attestations.

This caused 18.5% of blocks to be missed and network participation to drop to 75%.

The reason — an attempt to recreate old state for validating outdated data, which overloaded the nodes.

A temporary solution was the flag --disable-last-epoch-target, which removes the processing of old states.

A long-term fix has already been released in new versions, changing the validation logic for more efficient operation.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://prysm.offchainlabs.com/docs/misc/mainnet-postmortems/

🛡️ Top 10 DeFi Attacks in 2025: Old Code and Rounding Errors

In 2025, hackers exploited vulnerabilities in old code and rounding issues in DeFi, causing losses of hundreds of millions of dollars.

The largest attack targeted Balancer V2, resulting in a $121 million loss due to a token normalization error.

Other notable hacks include ResupplyFi ($9.8 million), Yearn Finance ($9 million), and KiloEx ($7.5 million), where traditional validation and access bugs allowed attackers to drain funds.

A special case was the hack via a new transient storage vulnerability in Synthetics Implemented Right, highlighting the rise of complex multi-vector attacks.

The year 2025 demonstrated: old contracts and rounding are the main security risks in Web3.

🔗 Details

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 350

### Text

👑 Dangers of Transferring Lamports on Solana

Transferring lamports to arbitrary accounts can silently fail due to the peculiarities of the rent model and write-demotion.

Even readable and rent-exempt accounts marked as executable or included in the reserved list may reject the transfer, breaking the program’s logic.

It’s better to avoid transfers to arbitrary addresses and instead keep funds in a PDA-based account to prevent eternal kings and bugs related to write-demotion.

This real issue has already affected projects and was discovered in Jito’s Bug Bounty.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://osec.io/blog/2025-05-14-king-of-the-sol/

🔗 Immutability in Cross-Chain Security

In cross-chain applications, data immutability is critical, as bridges (AMB) currently rely on a narrow set of validators and operate based on trust.

If validators are compromised, messages can be forged – for example, increasing the loan amount in DeFi protocols, as demonstrated in the example.

At present, there is no reliable on-chain solution to verify the integrity of cross-chain messages without centralized services.

The author plans to explore inexpensive and universal methods for data verification regardless of the AMB.

- **Views:** 403

### Text

🛡️ Critical DoS Vulnerability in Ethereum ZK Library gnark-crypto

During an audit of the Batcher Aligned Layer component, a vulnerability was discovered in gnark-crypto that allows causing a service crash by attempting to allocate up to 128 GB of memory when processing specially crafted input data.

The issue lies in the handling of vector lengths in ZK proof data — there is no size validation before allocation, leading to a DoS attack on services using this library.

The vulnerability affects all major curves (BN254, BLS12-381, and others) and has already been fixed in versions v0.18 and v0.19.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://fuzzinglabs.com/gnark-crypto-dos-cve/

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 313

### Text

🥪 Sandwich Attacks in DEXs

Sandwich attacks occur when an attacker places two transactions around your pending transaction, manipulating the price and capturing your profit.

The main cause is the lack of slippage protection, which allows your trade to execute at a worse price.

Adding a parameter for the minimum acceptable output amount in the swap function blocks these attacks — if the price changes too much, the transaction is automatically reverted.

Thus, simple slippage protection reduces vulnerability to mempool manipulation.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.cyfrin.io/blog/solodit-checklist-explained-11-sandwich-attacks

### Text

🌐 The Weak Link of Web2 in Web3 Security

Protecting smart contracts is important, but vulnerabilities often hide in Web2 systems — control panels, email, and cloud services.

Attacks start with DNS spoofing, script injection, or phishing — it’s not code hacking, but exploitation of traditional loopholes.

Within companies, complex architectures and imperfect authorization create multiple entry points for attackers.

A Web2 audit is not just a checklist review, but a deep analysis of the entire chain considering real threats and attacker behavior.

Without a comprehensive approach, the risk goes beyond smart contracts and affects the entire project environment.

Fuzzing is key to uncovering vulnerabilities in Solana programs.

Honggfuzz efficiently covers the code and quickly detects errors by checking balances and preventing incorrect fund transfers.
